Asymptotics of Polynomials Orthogonal with respect to a Logarithmic Weight This paper is a contribution to the Special Issue on Orthogonal Polynomials, Special Functions and Applications (OPSFA14). The full collection is available at https://www.emis.de/journals/SIGMA/OPSFA2017.html
Thomas Oliver CONWAY and Percy DEIFT

Abstract
In this paper we compute the asymptotic behavior of the recurrence coefficients for polynomials orthogonal with respect to a logarithmic weight w ⁡ ( x ) ​ d ​ x = log ⁡ 2 ​ k 1 − x ​ d ​ x w(x){\rm d}x=\log\frac{2k}{1-x}{\rm d}x on ( − 1 , 1 ) (-1,1) , k > 1 k>1 , and verify a conjecture of A. Magnus for these coefficients. We use Riemann–Hilbert/steepest-descent methods, but not in the standard way as there is no known parametrix for the Riemann–Hilbert problem in a neighborhood of the logarithmic singularity at x = 1 x=1 .



keywords
orthogonal polynomials; Riemann–Hilbert problems; recurrence coefficients; steepest descent method

AMS
33C47; 34E05; 34M50

1
Introduction

In this paper we consider the sequence of polynomials
\{p_{n}(x)\}
that are orthonormal with respect to a logarithmic measure
w(x){\rm d}x
on
[-1,1)
and 0 elsewhere

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}p_{i}(x)p_{j}(x)w(x){\rm d}x=\delta_{ij},

where

\displaystyle w(x)=\log\frac{2k}{1-x}.

We will only consider the case
k>1
, for which the measure is strictly positive on its support,
w(x)\geqslant c>0
for
x\in[-1,1)
.

The polynomials
\{p_{n}(x)\}
obey the well-known three term recurrence relation

\displaystyle xp_{n}(x)=b_{n}p_{n+1}(x)+a_{n}p_{n}(x)+b_{n-1}p_{n-1}(x),

where the recurrence coefficients
a_{n}\in\mathbb{R},b_{n}>0
are of particular interest.

Remark 1.1
.

Note that the notation for
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
in this paper is the same as the notation in
[
7
,
9
]
, but the reverse of the notation in
[
13
,
14
]
.

Logarithmic singularities like the ones exhibited in the above weight present various obstacles to the asymptotic analysis of the associated polynomials
\{p_{n}(x)\}
and their recurrence coefficients. The standard Riemann–Hilbert approach requires that explicit local solutions can be found in the neighborhood of singularities of the underlying measure for the associated Riemann–Hilbert problem. Because no such local solution is known near
x=1
for the logarithmic case, the standard approach cannot be applied directly.

The approach in this paper hinges instead on a comparison with the Legendre polynomials
\{\tilde{p}_{n}\}
which are orthogonal with respect to the weight

\displaystyle\tilde{w}(x)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ for $x\in[-1,1]$},\\
0,&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

and which have recurrence coefficients
\tilde{a}_{n}
,
\tilde{b}_{n}
. The asymptotics of
\tilde{a}_{n}
and
\tilde{b}_{n}
are well known. We have, from
[
13
]
,

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{n}=0,\qquad\tilde{b}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{16n^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{3}}\right).

The result of this comparison, and the main result of this paper is that

Theorem 1.2
.

As
n\to\infty
, the differences between the log-orthogonal coefficients and the Legendre coefficients have the following asymptotic behavior

\displaystyle a_{n}-\tilde{a}_{n}=\frac{2C}{(n\log n)^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}(\log n)^{3}}\right),

(1.1)

\displaystyle b_{n}-\tilde{b}_{n}=\frac{C}{(n\log n)^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}(\log n)^{3}}\right),

(1.2)

where
C=-\frac{3}{32}\approx-0.094
. In particular this implies that, as
n\to\infty
, the recurrence coefficients
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
have the following asymptotic behavior

\displaystyle a_{n}=\frac{2C}{(n\log n)^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}(\log n)^{3}}\right),

\displaystyle b_{n}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{16n^{2}}+\frac{C}{(n\log n)^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}(\log n)^{3}}\right).

This result, and more, was conjectured by Alphonse Magnus in
[
14
]
, up to the precise value for the constant
C
. We are indebted to his work, which provided the inspiration for the above result. For the weight
w(x)=-\log x
on
[0,1]
, in particular, Magnus conjectured that as
n\to\infty

\displaystyle a_{n}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{8n^{2}}-\frac{2C}{(n\log n)^{2}}+o\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}(\log n)^{2}}\right),

(1.3)

\displaystyle b_{n}=\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{32n^{2}}+\frac{C}{(n\log n)^{2}}+o\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}(\log n)^{2}}\right),

(1.4)

and on numerical grounds he conjectured further that
C\sim-0.044
. The weight
-\log x
on
[0,1]
corresponds to the case
k=1
for which our analysis is not yet complete. Nevertheless, our prelimary calculations confirm (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) with stronger estimates
O\big(\frac{1}{n^{2}(\log n)^{3}}\big)
on the error terms, and with the explicit value
-3/64
for
C
. As
-3/64\sim-0.047
this is close to Magnus’s conjectured value.

The proof proceeds as follows: The polynomials
\{p_{n}\}
have a well-known expression in terms of the solution
Y
of a Riemann–Hilbert problem due to Fokas–Its–Kitaev, see Section
3.1
. We follow a now standard series of transformations as in
[
7
,
13
]
, that transform the original Riemann–Hilbert problem for
Y
into a modified problem that is close to a Riemann–Hilbert problem whose asymptotics can be inferred directly.

Here is where our analysis diverges from the standard analysis. The standard analysis compares the modified Riemann–Hilbert problem to a model Riemann–Hilbert problem (parametrix) that explicitly solves the Riemann–Hilbert problem locally around the critical points at the edge of the measure. The new difficulty of our problem is that no explicit local solution is known near the logarithmic singularity. The main new feature of our analysis is that, for the problem at hand, no local solution is needed.

The strategy here is to compare the modified Riemann–Hilbert problem above to the corresponding modified Riemann–Hilbert problem for the Legendre case. In the standard approach, a comparison is made by examining the quotient of solutions to two Riemann–Hilbert problems as the solution to a Riemann–Hilbert problem in its own right. However, for the case at hand, the log singularity renders this approach ineffective. Instead we compare the problems through the operator theory that underlies Riemann–Hilbert problems, see Section
4
, or see
[
10
]
for more details.

In Theorem
4.4
we prove a general and very useful formula that allows for an effective comparison of two Riemann–Hilbert problems on the same contour. In our case, where we compare the log case with the Legendre case, this formula can be approximated by quantities solely related to the Legendre case, for which the asymptotics are well known, and this leads, eventually, to the formulas (
1.1
) and (
1.2
). It is a remarkable, and unexpected, development that the log problem can be approximated adequately by the Legendre problem.

The proof is laid out within this paper as follows:

•

In Section
2
, we prove several preliminary properties and estimates on the auxilliary functions that will arise in our analysis.

•

In Section
3
, we introduce the Riemann–Hilbert problem for the orthogonal polynomials and transform this problem into one which can be controlled as
n\to\infty
.

•

In Section
4
, we prove bounds, uniform in
n
, on the resolvent operators that are associated with the Riemann–Hilbert problem in the standard way and prove Theorem
4.4
.

•

In Section
5
, we calculate the asymptotics of the recurrence coefficients by combining the results of the previous sections with calculations derived from the known asymptotics of Legendre quanties.

•

The above calculations are given in Appendices
A
,
B
, and
C
.

Note that the case
k=1
corresponding to the weight
\log\frac{2}{1-x}
on the interval
[-1,1)
, for which
w(x)>0
on its support, but for which
w(x)\geqslant c>0
fails, is not considered in this paper. Recall that questions concerning orthogonal polynomials on the line with respect to a measure of compact support correspond to questions involving associated orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle, where the vanishing of the weight
\log\frac{2}{1-x}
at the point
-1
corresponds to a Fisher–Hartwig singularity for the related problem on the circle, see
[
8
]
. The methods in this paper do not extend immediately to cover this case, but will be addressed in a future paper.

This paper is in the line of questioning concerning the effect that singularities and zeroes in the measure have on the asymptotic behavior of orthogonal polynomials. Here we have in mind the extensive history of the problem dating originally back to the work of Szegő, proving the strong limit theorem for Toeplitz determinants for non-vanishing smooth weights on the circle without singularities
[
15
]
, and whose result has been extended to include a wide class of algebraic singularities, in particular, by the work of M.E. Fisher and R.E. Hartwig, see
[
8
]
for a history. Of additional interest is the recent work of Barry Simon et al. concerning sum laws of Szegő type where zeros of the form
e^{-1/x^{2}}
, for example, in the weight are investigated (see
[
2
]
for an up to date discussion). The logarithimic singularities explored in this paper are of practical interest in both physics and mathematics as described in
[
14
,
16
]
.

2
The
\boldsymbol{\phi}
function and the Szegő function

In this section, we introduce several functions central to our analysis and detail their properties.

2.1
The map
\boldsymbol{\phi}
and the Szegő function
\boldsymbol{F}

The first part of our work closely follows the work from the papers
[
9
,
13
,
17
]
, and uses the Riemann–Hilbert approach to orthogonal polynomials, first put forward by Fokas, Its, and Kitaev
[
11
]
. We apply similar sets of transformations to the Riemann–Hilbert problems associated with the weights
w
and
\tilde{w}
,

\displaystyle Y\to T\to Q,\qquad\tilde{Y}\to\tilde{T}\to\tilde{Q}.

The problems
Q
and
\tilde{Q}
are then close in a “certain” sense, allowing the comparison. The problem
\tilde{Q}
can be asymptotically solved as in
[
13
]
, as repeated in the appendix, also see Section
B.1
.

In the calculations that follow
\big(z^{2}-1\big)^{1/2}
refers to the branch of the square root of
z^{2}-1
that is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
and is positive for
z>1
. For
z\in(-1,1)
,
\big(z^{2}-1\big)^{1/2}_{\pm}
refers to the boundary value of
\big(z^{2}-1\big)
as
z^{\prime}\to z
with
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
respectively,
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}=\{z=x\pm iy\,|\,x\in\mathbb{R},\,y>0\}
. Additionally, when
x>0
,
\sqrt{x}
will refer to the positive square root of
x
.

The map

\displaystyle\phi(z)=z+\big(z^{2}-1\big)^{1/2},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]

plays a key role in the transformations
Y\to T
and
\tilde{Y}\to\tilde{T}
. In particular
\phi
plays the role of normalizing these problems at infinity. The function
\phi
has the following properties,

Proposition 2.1
.

The function
\phi
, and its boundary values,
\phi_{\pm}
, on the interval
[-1,1]
have the following properties:

\displaystyle 0)\quad\phi(z)\text{ is analytic for $z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]$},

(2.1)

\displaystyle 1)\quad\phi(z)=2z+O\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)\text{ as $z\to\infty$},

(2.2)

\displaystyle 2)\quad\phi(z)=1+\sqrt{2}(z-1)^{1/2}+O(z-1)\text{ as $z\to 1$},

(2.3)

\displaystyle 3)\quad\phi(z)=-1+\sqrt{2}i(z+1)^{1/2}+O(z+1)\text{ as $z\to-1$},

(2.4)

\displaystyle 4)\quad\phi(\overline{z})=\overline{\phi(z)},

(2.5)

\displaystyle 5)\quad|\phi(z)|>1,

(2.6)

\displaystyle 6)\quad\phi_{\pm}(x)=x\pm i\sqrt{1-x^{2}},

(2.7)

\displaystyle 7)\quad\phi_{+}(x)\phi_{-}(x)=1,

(2.8)

\displaystyle 8)\quad|\phi_{\pm}(x)|=1.

(2.9)

The function
(z-1)^{1/2}
in (
2.3
) is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\backslash(-\infty,1]
and positive for
z>1
and the function
(z+1)^{1/2}
in (
2.4
) is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\backslash(-1,\infty]
and is positive imaginary from
z<-1
.

Proof.

We will prove statement (
2.6
); the remaining statements follow directly from the definition of
\phi
.

First note that for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle\phi(z)\times\big(z-\big(z^{2}-1\big)^{1/2}\big)=z^{2}-\big(z^{2}-1\big)=1.

Therefore
\phi(z)\neq 0
. The function
\frac{1}{\phi}
is therefore analytic in the region
\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1.1]
. By property (
2.2
), we see that
\frac{1}{\phi}(z)\to 0
as
z\to\infty
, and by property (
2.9
), we see that
\big|\frac{1}{\phi}(z)\big|\to 1
as
z\to[-1,1]
. Therefore, the maximum modulus principle guarantees that
\big|\frac{1}{\phi}(z)\big|<1
for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
and so
|\phi(z)|>1
in the same region.
∎

For the log-orthogonal polynomial case, we make use of the associated Szegő function
F
similar to the non-Legendre cases analyzed in
[
13
]
,

\displaystyle F(z)=\exp\left(\big(z^{2}-1\big)^{1/2}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\log w(s)}{\big(s^{2}-1\big)^{1/2}_{+}}\frac{{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts}{s-z}\right),

where

\displaystyle{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts:=\frac{{\rm d}s}{2\pi i}

and similarly
{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptt,{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz,\dots
mean
\frac{{\rm d}t}{2\pi i},\frac{{\rm d}z}{2\pi i},\dots
. In the calculations that follow we shall use the following lemma repeatedly without further comment:

Lemma 2.2
.

The function
w(s)=\log\frac{2k}{1-s}
has an analytic extension from
[-1,1)
to
\mathbb{C}\backslash[1,\infty)
where
\log
takes on its principle value.

The function
L(s):=\log w(s)
has an analytic extension from
(-1,1)
to
\mathbb{C}\backslash((-\infty,1-2k]\cup[1,\infty))
, where again
\log
takes its principle value.

Proof.

The proof is left to the reader.
∎

Proposition 2.3
.

The function
F
, and its boundaray values,
F_{\pm}
, on the interval
[-1,1]
have the following properties:

\displaystyle 0)\quad F(z)\text{ is analytic for $z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]$},

(2.10)

\displaystyle 1)\quad F(z)=F_{\infty}+\frac{F_{1}}{z}+O\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)\text{ as $z\to\infty$},\text{ for suitable constants $F_{\infty},F_{1}\in\mathbb{R}$},

(2.11)

\displaystyle 2)\quad\frac{F^{2}}{w}(z)=1\mp\frac{i\pi}{w(z)}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{2w^{2}(z)}+O\left(\frac{1}{w^{3}(z)}\right)\text{ uniformly as $z\to 1$},

(2.12)

\displaystyle 3)\quad\frac{F^{2}}{w}(z)=1+O(|z+1|^{1/2})\text{ uniformly as $z\to-1$},

(2.13)

\displaystyle 4)\quad F(\overline{z})=\overline{F(z)},

(2.14)

\displaystyle 5)\quad F_{+}(x)F_{-}(x)=w(x).

(2.15)

Proof.

With the exception of statements (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) of Proposition
2.1
, the above statements are straightforward to prove. Statements (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) are proven in Appendix
A.1
as Proposition
A.1
and Proposition
A.3
.
∎

Additionally, as a consequence of Proposition
2.3
, the Szegő function satisfies.

Proposition 2.4
.

\displaystyle\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(x)+\frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(x)-2=-\frac{3\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\frac{2k}{|x-1|}}+O\left(\frac{1}{\log^{3}|1-x|}\right)

for
x>1
as
x\to 1
.

Proof.

As the estimate in statement (
2.12
) of Proposition
2.3
is uniform in
0<|\theta|<\pi
, we can compute for
x>1
,
x\to 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(x)+\frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(x)-2=-\frac{i\pi}{w_{+}(x)}+\frac{i\pi}{w_{-}(x)}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\frac{2k}{|x-1|}}+O\left(\frac{1}{\log^{3}|1-x|}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(x) + \frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(x) - 2}{}=\frac{i\pi(-w_{-}+w_{+})}{w_{+}(x)w_{-}(x)}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\frac{2k}{|x-1|}}+O\left(\frac{1}{\log^{3}|1-x|}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(x) + \frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(x) - 2}{}=-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{w_{+}(x)w_{-}(x)}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\frac{2k}{|x-1|}}+O\left(\frac{1}{\log^{3}|1-x|}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(x) + \frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(x) - 2}{}=-\frac{3\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\frac{2k}{|x-1|}}+O\left(\frac{1}{\log^{3}|1-x|}\right)

as
x\to 1
.
∎

We will also need the following technical estimate on the difference
\frac{F^{2}}{w_{\pm}}(1+r)-\frac{F^{2}}{w_{\pm}}(1+\tilde{r})
for
r,\tilde{r}>0
suitably small and close together. This estimate is needed to estimate the integral in Proposition
5.4
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Fix
R>0
and suppose
r,\tilde{r}>0
obey

\displaystyle r,\tilde{r}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right),\qquad n\left(\frac{r}{\tilde{r}}-1\right)\in[-R,R].

Then

\displaystyle\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(1+r)-\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(1+\tilde{r})+\frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(1+r)-\frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(1+\tilde{r})=O_{R}\left(\frac{1}{n\log^{3}n}\right).

Proof.

See Proposition
A.4
.
∎

3
The Riemann–Hilbert approach

In this section we define the main Riemann–Hilbert problem and transform the problem into a form which is amenable to asymptotic analysis.

Remark 3.1
.

The notation
O_{n}
is used as follows. For matrices

\displaystyle A(z)=A^{(n)}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}A_{11}^{(n)}(z)&A^{(n)}_{12}(z)\\
A_{21}^{(n)}(z)&A^{(n)}_{22}(z)\end{matrix}\right),\qquad B(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}B_{11}(z)&B_{12}(z)\\
B_{21}(z)&B_{22}(z)\end{matrix}\right)

we say
A(z)=O_{n}(B(z))
as
z\to z_{0}
if, for each pair
i,j=1,2
, and for any
n\geqslant 0
, there exists
\varepsilon
and
c_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\big|A^{(n)}_{ij}(z)\big|\leqslant c_{n}|B_{ij}(z)|

for all
|z-z_{0}|\leqslant\varepsilon
. Similarly, for scalar functions
A_{n}(z)
and
B(z)
, we say that
A_{n}(z)=O(B(z))
if there exists
c_{n}
such that

\displaystyle|A_{n}(z)|\leqslant c_{n}|B(z)|

3.1
Riemann–Hilbert problem for
\boldsymbol{Y}

We will denote by
\pi_{n}(x)=x^{n}+\cdots
,
\tilde{\pi}_{n}=x^{n}+\cdots
the monic, orthogonal polynomials associated with the weights
w,\tilde{w}
respectively. The polynomials
\{\pi_{n}\}
are described by the following Riemann–Hilbert problem introduced by Fokas, Its, and Kitaev
[
11
]
(see also
[
7
]
) for a 2
\times
2 matrix valued function
Y(z)=Y^{(n)}(z)
:

(a)

\displaystyle Y^{(n)}(z)\text{ is analytic for $z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]$}.

(3.1)

(b)

Y^{(n)}
has boundary values,
Y_{\pm}:=\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to x,z\in\mathbb{C}_{\pm}\end{subarray}}Y(z)
, for
x\in\mathbb{R}
pointwise almost
everywhere and in the following
L^{2}
sense:

\displaystyle\text{for any $R>1$},\qquad\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{-R}^{R}|Y(x\pm i\epsilon)-Y_{\pm}(x)|^{2}{\rm d}x=0

and

\displaystyle Y^{(n)}_{+}(x)=Y^{(n)}_{-}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}1&w(x)\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\qquad\text{for $x\in(-1,1)$}.

(3.2)

(c)

Y^{(n)}
has the following asymptotics as
z\to\infty

\displaystyle Y^{(n)}(z)=\left(I+O_{n}\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)\right)\left(\begin{matrix}z^{n}&0\\
0&z^{-n}\end{matrix}\right).

(3.3)

Of course, for
|x|>1
,
Y_{+}(x)=Y_{-}(x)=Y(x)
.

Define the Cauchy operator,
C_{[-1,1]}
by

\displaystyle C_{[-1,1]}f=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{f(s)}{s-z}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

and let
C_{[-1,1]}^{\pm}
denote its boundary values from
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
respectively. We give a brief summary of the properties of the Cauchy operator and its boundary values on general contours in Section
4.2
.

Proposition 3.2

(Fokas–Its–Kitaev)
.

Let
\gamma_{n}>0
be the leading coefficient of
p_{n}
, the orthonormal polynomials with respect to the weight
w
,
p_{n}=\gamma_{n}\pi_{n}
. The function

\displaystyle Y^{(n)}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}\pi_{n}(z)&(C_{[-1,1]}\pi_{n}w)(z)\\
2\pi i\gamma_{n-1}^{2}\pi_{n-1}(s)&2\pi i\gamma_{n-1}^{2}(C_{[-1,1]}\pi_{n-1}w)(z)\end{matrix}\right)

(3.4)

uniquely solves the RHP for
Y^{(n)}
.

Proof.

Aside from some special attention that must be paid to the
L^{2}
sense in which the boundary values
Y_{\pm}
are achieved, the proof proceeds as in
[
7
]
. The
L^{2}
condition in (
3.2
) plays the following key role in proving uniqueness:

Note that, by the
L^{2}
condition in (
3.2
), for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{a}^{b}\left|\det Y(x\pm i\epsilon)-\det Y_{\pm}(x)\right|{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{a}^{b}\left|Y_{11}Y_{22}(x\pm i\epsilon)-(Y_{11}Y_{22})_{\pm}(x)-\left(Y_{12}Y_{21}(x\pm i\epsilon)-(Y_{12}Y_{21})_{\pm}(x)\right)\right|{\rm d}x=0.

However, the jump condition in (
3.2
) implies
\det Y_{+}(x)=\det Y_{-}(x)
for almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{a}^{b}\det Y(x+i\epsilon)=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{a}^{b}\det Y(x-i\epsilon)

and it follows, by standard calculations, that
\int_{C}\det Y(z){\rm d}z=0
for all closed contours
C\subset\mathbb{C}
. Hence
\det Y(z)
is entire by Morera’s theorem. Condition (
3.3
) and Liouville’s theorem then implies that
\det Y(z)=1
for all
z
. In particular,
Y(z)
is invertible for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
.

Let
\tilde{Y}
be a second solution to the RHP (
3.1
)–(
3.3
) and define
H(z)=\tilde{Y}(z)Y^{-1}(z)
, and repeating the same argument, we find that
H(z)
is entire, and
H(z)\to I
as
z\to\infty
and hence
H(z)=1
for all
z
and therefore
Y=\tilde{Y}
.

We must also demonstrate that the function
Y^{(n)}(z)
defined in (
3.4
) achieves its boundary values in the aforementioned
L^{2}
sense. A standard argument as in
[
13
]
shows that

(d)

Y^{(n)}
has the following uniform asymptotics as
z\to-1
,
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]

\displaystyle Y^{(n)}(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}1&\log(|z+1|)\\
1&\log(|z+1|)\end{matrix}\right).

(3.5)

0
z-1=re^{i\theta}
C_{1}
C_{2}
2e^{i\theta}
2

Figure 1:
Definition of
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
.

We must also analyze
Y(z)
for
z
near 1. Let
z=1+re^{i\theta}
where
r>0
and
-\pi<\theta<\pi
, and recall from Lemma
2.2
that
w(s)
has to analytic extension in
\mathbb{C}\backslash[1,\infty)
. The following estimate holds uniformly for
-\pi<\theta<\pi
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\pi_{n}(s)w(s)}{s-z}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|=\left|\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\pi_{n}(s)w(s)}{s-1-re^{i\theta}}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|=\left|\int_{0}^{2}\frac{\pi_{n}(1-s)w(1-s)}{s+re^{i\theta}}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|

\displaystyle\hphantom{\left|\int_{-1}^1 \frac{\pi_{n}(s) w(s)}{s-z} {\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|}{}=\left|\int_{C_{1}}\frac{\pi_{n}(1-s)w(1-s)}{s+re^{i\theta}}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+\int_{C_{2}}\frac{\pi_{n}(1-s)w(1-s)}{s+re^{i\theta}}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|,

where
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
are depicted in Fig.
1
. The integrand is clearly bounded over
C_{2}
is clearly analytic and hence the integral of
C_{2}
is
O(1)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{C_{1}}\frac{\pi_{n}(1-s)w(1-s)}{s+re^{i\theta}}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|\leqslant c_{n}\int_{C_{1}}\left|\frac{w(1-s)}{s+re^{i\theta}}\right||{\rm d}s|.

Making the change of variables
s=re^{i\theta}t
,

\displaystyle c_{n}\int_{C_{1}}\left|\frac{w(1-s)}{s+re^{i\theta}}\right||{\rm d}s|=c_{n}\int_{0}^{2/r}\left|\frac{w(1-re^{i\theta}t)}{re^{i\theta}t+re^{i\theta}}\right|r{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\hphantom{c_n \int_{C_1}\left|\frac{w(1-s)}{s + re^{i\theta}}\right||{\rm d}s|}{}=c_{n}\int_{0}^{2/r}\left|\frac{w(1-re^{i\theta}t)}{t+1}\right|{\rm d}t=c_{n}\int_{0}^{2/r}\left|\frac{\log\frac{re^{i\theta}t}{2k}}{t+1}\right|{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\hphantom{c_n \int_{C_1}\left|\frac{w(1-s)}{s + re^{i\theta}}\right||{\rm d}s|}{}\leqslant c_{n}\int_{0}^{2/r}\left|\frac{\log t}{t+1}\right|{\rm d}t+c_{n}\log r\int_{0}^{2/r}\left|\frac{1}{t+1}\right|{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\hphantom{c_n \int_{C_1}\left|\frac{w(1-s)}{s + re^{i\theta}}\right||{\rm d}s|}{}\leqslant c_{n}\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{\log t}{t+1}\right|{\rm d}t+c_{n}\int_{1}^{2/r}\left|\frac{\log t}{t+1}\right|{\rm d}t+O_{n}\big(\log^{2}r\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{c_n \int_{C_1}\left|\frac{w(1-s)}{s + re^{i\theta}}\right||{\rm d}s|}{}\leqslant c_{n}\log r\int_{1}^{2/r}\left|\frac{1}{t+1}\right|{\rm d}t+O_{n}\big(\log^{2}t\big)=O_{n}\big(\log^{2}r\big)

proving

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\pi_{n}(s)w(s)}{s-z}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|=O_{n}\big(\log^{2}|z-1|\big)

uniformly for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
, as
z\to 1
. Therefore,

(e)

Y^{(n)}
has the following uniform asymptotics as
z\to 1
,
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]

\displaystyle Y^{(n)}(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}1&\log^{2}(|z-1|)\\
1&\log^{2}(|z-1|)\end{matrix}\right).

(3.6)

Lastly, note that the function
Y^{(n)}(z)
clearly is continuous up to the boundary for all
x\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{-1,1\}
from the properties of the Cauchy operator. Therefore, combining (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), it is clear that
Y
achieves its boundary values
Y_{\pm}
in the
L^{2}
sense described in (
3.2
).
∎

Let
\sigma_{3}
refer to the 3rd Pauli matrix

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{matrix}\right).

The recurrence coefficients
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
associated with the polynomials
\{\pi_{n}\}
have the following expressions in terms of the solution
Y^{(n)}(z)
:

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
Y^{(n)}_{1}
be the coefficient of
\frac{1}{z}
in the Laurent expansion
Y^{(n)}(z)z^{-n\sigma_{3}}=I+\frac{Y^{(n)}_{1}}{z}+O\big(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\big)
as
z\to\infty
. Then

\displaystyle a_{n}=\big(Y_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(Y_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11},\qquad b_{n-1}^{2}=\big(Y_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big(Y_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21},

and

\displaystyle 1=\big(Y^{(n)}_{1}\big)_{12}\big(Y^{(n+1)}_{1}\big)_{21}.

Proof.

See
[
7
]
.
∎

References for the calculations that follow are

,

,

,

]
.

3.2
The transformation
\boldsymbol{Y\to T}

The first step in the asymptotic analysis of
Y
, is to provide a reformulation of the RHP described by (
3.1
)–(
3.3
) in terms of a RHP normalized at infinity, and now with a bounded, but highly oscillatory, jump function.

Remark 3.4
.

In the standard Riemann–Hilbert theory, the RHP is converted to a problem involving a bounded singular operator on the contour. The replacement of the unbounded jump
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&w\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
with a bounded jump makes it possible to apply Riemann–Hilbert theory directly.

Recall the definitions of
\phi
and
F
from Section
2
and define

\displaystyle T(z)=\left(\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}Y(z)\left(\frac{F(z)}{\phi^{n}(z)}\right)^{\sigma_{3}},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1].

(3.7)

Since
Y
,
\phi
, and
F
are all analytic in the region
\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
,
T
clearly satisfies the same analyticity condition (
3.1
) as
Y
.

For
x\in(-1,1)

\displaystyle T_{+}(x)=\left(\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}Y_{+}(x)\left(\frac{F_{+}(x)}{\phi^{n}_{+}(x)}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}=\left(\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}Y_{-}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}1&w(x)\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\left(\frac{F_{+}(x)}{\phi^{n}_{+}(x)}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{T_+(x)}{}=T_{-}(x)\left(\frac{F_{-}(x)}{\phi^{n}_{-}(x)}\right)^{-\sigma_{3}}\left(\begin{matrix}1&w(x)\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\left(\frac{F_{+}(x)}{\phi^{n}_{+}(x)}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{T_+(x)}{}=T_{-}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}\dfrac{F_{+}}{F_{-}}\left(\dfrac{\phi_{-}}{\phi_{+}}\right)^{n}&\dfrac{\phi_{-}^{n}\phi_{+}^{n}}{F_{+}F_{-}}w\vskip 2.84526pt\\
0&\dfrac{F_{-}}{F_{+}}\left(\dfrac{\phi_{+}}{\phi_{-}}\right)^{n}\end{matrix}\right)=T_{-}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}\dfrac{F_{+}^{2}}{w}\phi_{+}^{-2n}&1\\
0&\dfrac{F_{-}^{2}}{w}\phi_{-}^{-2n}\end{matrix}\right),

where we have used property (
2.8
) of Proposition
2.1
and property (
2.15
) of Proposition
2.3
.
Combining property (
2.2
) of Proposition
2.1
and property (
2.11
) of Proposition
2.3
with (
3.7
), we see that

\displaystyle T(z)=I+O_{n}\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)\qquad\text{as $z\to\infty$}

Finally by properties (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) of Proposition
2.1
and properties (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) of Proposition
2.3
and by conditions (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) of the RHP for
Y
, we see that

\displaystyle T(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}1&\log(|z+1|)\\
1&\log(|z+1|)\end{matrix}\right)\qquad\text{as $z\to-1$},

\displaystyle T(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}\log^{1/2}(|z-1|)&\log^{3/2}(|z-1|)\\
\log^{1/2}(|z-1|)&\log^{3/2}(|z-1|)\end{matrix}\right)\qquad\text{as $z\to 1$}.

To summarize,
T(z)
solves the following standard RHP:

(a)

T(z)
is analytic for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
,

(b)

T^{(n)}
has continuous boundary values for
x\in(-1,1)
, which we denote by
T^{(n)}_{+}(x)
and
T^{(n)}_{-}(x)
and

\displaystyle T_{+}(x)=T_{-}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}\dfrac{F_{+}^{2}}{w}\phi_{+}^{-2n}&1\\
0&\dfrac{F_{-}^{2}}{w}\phi_{-}^{-2n}\end{matrix}\right)\qquad\text{for $x\in(-1,1)$},

(3.8)

(c)

T(z)
has the following asymptotics as
z\to\infty

\displaystyle T(z)=I+O_{n}\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)\qquad\text{as $z\to\infty$},

(d)

T^{(n)}
has the following asymptotics as
z\to-1

\displaystyle T(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}1&\log(|z+1|)\\
1&\log(|z+1|)\end{matrix}\right),

(e)

T^{(n)}
has the following asymptotics as
z\to 1

\displaystyle T(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}\log^{1/2}(|z-1|)&\log^{3/2}(|z-1|)\\
\log^{1/2}(|z-1|)&\log^{3/2}(|z-1|)\end{matrix}\right).

It follows from the definitions of
T
(
3.7
) and the fact that
\det(Y)\equiv 1
that
\det(T)\equiv 1
.

Remark 3.5
.

Properties (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) of Proposition
2.3
imply that
\frac{F^{2}}{w}
is bounded uniformly as we approach the interval
[-1,1]
and therefore the jump matrix for
T
in (
3.8
) is indeed bounded.

3.3
The transformation
\boldsymbol{T\to Q}

The transformations in these sections are well known to those familiar with the Riemann–Hilbert approach to orthogonal polynomials. The next transformation is a slight modification of the typical “lens” transformation. The difference is that it collects factors in the upper and lower lips near the singularity at 1. For our particular weight, these factors cancel each other to leading order, due to Proposition
2.4
. We “deform” the RHP on
[-1,1]
to a problem on the oriented contour
\Sigma
as depicted in Fig.
2
.

-1
1
1+\delta
\Sigma_{1}
\Sigma_{2}
\Omega_{0}
\Omega_{1}
\Omega_{2}
+
-
+
-
+
-
-
+

Figure 2:
Definition of
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}\cup[-1,1]\cup[1+\delta,1]
.

The transformation in this section is based on the factorization of the jump matrix of
T
in (
3.8
) into a product of 3 matrices on
(-1,1)
:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\dfrac{F_{+}^{2}}{w}\phi_{+}^{-2n}&1\\
0&\dfrac{F_{-}^{2}}{w}\phi_{-}^{-2n}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\dfrac{F_{-}^{2}}{w}\phi_{-}^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\dfrac{F_{+}^{2}}{w}\phi_{+}^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right).

Since
F
,
\phi
are analytic in
\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
, and, by Lemma
2.2
,
\log\frac{2k}{1-s}
has a unique analytic continuation to
\mathbb{C}\backslash[1,\infty)
, the matrix valued function

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\dfrac{F^{2}}{w}\phi^{-2n}(z)&1\end{matrix}\right)

is an analytic function on
\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,\infty)
and, by Remark
3.5
and Proposition
2.1
is continuous up to the boundary. Now define

\displaystyle Q(z)=\begin{cases}T(z)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{0}$},\\
T(z)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
-\dfrac{F^{2}}{w}\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{1}$},\\
T(z)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\dfrac{F^{2}}{w}\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{2}$}.\end{cases}

(3.9)

A straightforward calculations shows
Q=Q^{(n)}
solves the following RHP:

(a)

Q(z)
is analytic for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\Sigma
,

(b)

Q
has continuous boundary values for
s\in\Sigma\backslash\{-1,1\}
and

Q_{+}(s)=Q_{-}(s)v_{\Sigma}(s)
,
s\in\Sigma
, where

\displaystyle v_{\Sigma}(s)=\begin{cases}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\dfrac{F^{2}}{w}\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}$},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\left(\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}+\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}\right)\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in[1,1+\delta]$},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in[-1,1]$},\end{cases}

(c)

as
z\to\infty

\displaystyle Q(z)=I+\frac{Q_{1}}{z}+O_{n}\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right),

(d)

Q
has the following asymptotics as
z\to-1

\displaystyle Q(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}\log(|z+1|)&\log(|z+1|)\\
\log(|z+1|)&\log(|z+1|)\end{matrix}\right),

(3.10)

(e)

Q
has the following asymptotics as
z\to 1

\displaystyle Q(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}\log^{3/2}(|z-1|)&\log^{3/2}(|z-1|)\\
\log^{3/2}(|z-1|)&\log^{3/2}(|z-1|)\end{matrix}\right).

(3.11)

The endpoint conditions in (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) guarantee that the solution
Q
to the above RHP achieves its boundary values in the
L^{2}
sense. The same argument as in Proposition
3.2
proves that this solution is unique. Therefore, any solution to the above RHP must be the solution we obtain from the RHP for
Y
through the transformations (
3.7
) and (
3.9
).

3.4
The associated Legendre problem,
\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}

The same steps as in Section
3.3
can be repeated for the problem with the Legendre weight
\tilde{w}(s)=1
,
s\in[-1,1]
, in which case all of the above calculations hold true provided we take
F\equiv 1
.

This results in the following RHP on
\Sigma
for
\tilde{Q}=\tilde{Q}^{(n)}
:

(a)

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}(z)\text{ is analytic for $z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\Sigma$.}

(3.12)

(b)

\tilde{Q}
has continuous boundary values for
x\in\Sigma\backslash\{-1,1\}
and

\tilde{Q}_{+}(s)=\tilde{Q}_{-}(s)v_{\Sigma}(s)

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}(s)=\begin{cases}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}$},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
2\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in[1,1+\delta]$},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in[-1,1]$},\end{cases}

(3.13)

(c)

as
z\to\infty

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}(z)=I+\frac{\tilde{Q}_{1}}{z}+O_{n}\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right),

(d)

\tilde{Q}
has the following asymptotics as
z\to-1

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}\log(|z+1|)&\log(|z+1|)\\
\log(|z+1|)&\log(|z+1|)\end{matrix}\right),

(e)

\tilde{Q}
has the following asymptotics as
z\to 1

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}\log(|z-1|)&\log(|z-1|)\\
\log(|z-1|)&\log(|z-1|)\end{matrix}\right).

(3.14)

3.5
A formula for recurrence coefficients

Proposition 3.6
.

The following formulas hold for the recurrence coefficients in terms of the modified RHPs
Q
,
\tilde{Q}
,

\displaystyle a_{n}-\tilde{a}_{n}=\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11}\big),

\displaystyle b_{n-1}^{2}-\tilde{b}_{n-1}^{2}=\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big)\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{b_{n-1}^2 - \tilde{b}_{n-1}^2 =}{}+\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big[\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}-\big(\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big)\big].

Proof.

Note, from Proposition
3.3
, that the recurrence coefficients
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
are related to the RHP for
Y
via the formulas

\displaystyle a_{n}=\big(Y^{(n)}_{1}\big)_{11}-\big(Y^{(n+1)}_{1}\big)_{11},

(3.15)

\displaystyle b_{n-1}^{2}=\big(Y^{(n)}_{1}\big)_{12}\big(Y^{(n)}_{1}\big)_{21},

(3.16)

and, again from Proposition
3.3
,
Y
satisfies the following symmetry relation:

\displaystyle 1=\big(Y^{(n)}_{1}\big)_{12}\big(Y^{(n+1)}_{1}\big)_{21}.

(3.17)

Now for
z
sufficiently large (outside of the contour
\Sigma
)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}Y(z)\left(\frac{F(z)}{\phi^{n}(z)}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}=Q(z)

and

\displaystyle\left(I+\frac{Y_{1}}{z}+O_{n}\big(z^{-2}\big)\right)z^{n\sigma_{3}}=\left(\frac{F_{\infty}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}\left(I+\frac{Q_{1}}{z}+O_{n}\big(z^{-2}\big)\right)\left(\frac{\phi^{n}(z)}{F(z)}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}.

By Propositions
2.1
and
2.3

\displaystyle\phi(z)=2z+O_{n}\big(z^{-1}\big),

\displaystyle F(z)=F_{\infty}+\frac{F_{1}}{z}+O_{n}\big(z^{-2}\big).

Hence

\displaystyle\frac{\phi^{n}(z)}{F(z)}=\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}+\frac{F_{1}}{z}+O_{n}\big(z^{-2}\big)}z^{n}+O_{n}\big(z^{n-2}\big)=\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}}z^{n}-\frac{2^{n}F_{1}}{F_{\infty}^{2}}z^{n-1}+O_{n}\big(z^{n-2}\big),

\displaystyle\frac{F(z)}{\phi^{n}(z)}=\frac{F_{\infty}}{2^{n}z^{n}+O_{n}\big(z^{n-2}\big)}+\frac{F_{1}}{2^{n}z^{n+1}+O_{n}(z^{n-1})}+O_{n}\big(z^{n-2}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\frac{\phi^{n}(z)}{F(z)}}{}=\frac{F_{\infty}}{2^{n}}z^{-n}+\frac{F_{1}}{2^{n}}z^{-n-1}+O_{n}\big(z^{-n-2}\big).

So

\displaystyle\left(I+\frac{Y_{1}}{z}+O_{n}\big(z^{-2}\right)\big)z^{n\sigma_{3}}=\left(\frac{F_{\infty}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}\left(I+\frac{Q_{1}}{z}+O_{n}\big(z^{-2}\right)\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\left(I + \frac{Y_{1}}{z} + O_n\big(z^{-2}\right)\big)z^{n\sigma_3} =}{}\times\left(\left(\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}+\frac{1}{z}\left(\begin{matrix}-\frac{2^{n}F_{1}}{F_{\infty}^{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{F_{1}}{2^{n}}\end{matrix}\right)+O_{n}\big(z^{-2}\right)\big)z^{n\sigma_{3}},

\displaystyle\frac{Y_{1}}{z}=\frac{1}{z}\left(\frac{F_{\infty}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}Q_{1}\left(\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}+\frac{1}{z}\left(\begin{matrix}-\dfrac{F_{1}}{F_{\infty}}&0\\
0&\dfrac{F_{1}}{F_{\infty}}\end{matrix}\right),

\displaystyle Y_{1}=\left(\frac{F_{\infty}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}Q_{1}\left(\frac{2^{n}}{F_{\infty}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}+\left(\begin{matrix}-\dfrac{F_{1}}{F_{\infty}}&0\\
0&\dfrac{F_{1}}{F_{\infty}}\end{matrix}\right),

and so the formula for the recurrence coefficients in (
3.15
)–(
3.16
) can be expressed in terms of the RHP for
Q
by

\displaystyle a_{n}=\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11},\qquad b_{n-1}^{2}=\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}.

The symmetry consideration (
3.17
) becomes

\displaystyle 1=4\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}

and so

\displaystyle a_{n}=\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11},

\displaystyle b_{n-1}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}=\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big).

The same formulas hold for
\tilde{a}_{n}
and
\tilde{b}_{n-1}^{2}
, replacing
Q_{1}
,
Y_{1}
with
\tilde{Q}_{1}
,
\tilde{Y}_{1}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle a_{n}-\tilde{a}_{n}=\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11}\big)

\displaystyle b_{n-1}^{2}-\tilde{b}_{n-1}^{2}=\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big)-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big(\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{b_{n-1}^2 - \tilde{b}_{n-1}^2}{}=\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big)\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{b_{n-1}^2 - \tilde{b}_{n-1}^2=}{}+\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big[\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}-\big(\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big)\big]

as desired.
∎

4
A comparison of resolvents

In this section we develop the machinery that will allow us to compare two Riemann–Hilbert problems using non-local information.

4.1
Comparing Riemann–Hilbert problem through the Cauchy transform

For a bounded, oriented, rectifiable contour
\Sigma
, consider
C_{\Sigma}
acting on
L^{p}(\Sigma)
,
1\leqslant p<\infty
, as follows

\displaystyle(C_{\Sigma}f)(z)=\int_{\Sigma}\frac{f(s)}{s-z}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts,

where recall
{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts=\frac{{\rm d}s}{2\pi i}
. For almost all
z\in\Sigma
, the limits

\displaystyle(C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}f)(z):=\lim_{z^{\prime}\to z^{\pm}}\int_{\sigma}\frac{f(s)}{s-z^{\prime}}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

exist. Furthermore,

\displaystyle(C^{+}f)(z)-(C^{-}f)(z)=f(z)

for almost all
z\in\Sigma
, and

\displaystyle(C^{+}f)(z)+(C^{-}f)(z)=iHf(z),

where
H
is the Hilbert transform

\displaystyle Hf(z):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Sigma\\
|z-z^{\prime}|>\epsilon\end{subarray}}\frac{f(z^{\prime})}{z^{\prime}-z}\frac{{\rm d}z^{\prime}}{\pi},

which exists for almost all
z\in\Sigma
. An oriented, rectifiable contour
\Sigma
is called Carleson (or Ahlfors–David) if

\displaystyle K_{\Sigma}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\Sigma\\
r>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|U_{r}(z)\cap\Sigma\right|}{r}<\infty,

where
U_{r}(z)
denotes a ball of radius
r
centered at
z
.

The fundamental theorem, starting with the work of Calderon
[
3
]
, continuing through the work of Coifman, Meyer, and McIntosh
[
5
]
, and culminating in the work of Guy David
[
6
]
, is the following: The operators
C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}
are bounded from
L^{2}(\Sigma)\to L^{2}(\Sigma)
(and in fact in
L^{p}(\Sigma)
for all
1<p<\infty
) if and only if
\Sigma
is Carleson. Note, in particular, that the contour in this paper is clearly Carleson.

We will say that a pair of functions
A_{\pm}\in L^{p}(\Sigma)
belongs to
\partial C(L^{p}(\Sigma))
iff
A_{\pm}=C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}f
for some (unique)
f\in L^{p}(\Sigma)
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a Carleson curve, and let
A_{\pm}\in I+\partial C(L^{p}(\Sigma))
and
B_{\pm}\in I+\partial C(L^{q}(\Sigma))
for
1<p,q<\infty
where
1\geqslant\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}
. Then if
A(z)
,
B(z)
are the extensions of
A_{\pm}
,
B_{\pm}
respectively, then
(AB)(z)
is the extension of

\displaystyle(AB)_{\pm}\in I+\partial C\big(L^{p}(\Sigma)+L^{q}(\Sigma)+L^{r}(\Sigma)\big).

If
\Sigma
is compact, then

\displaystyle(AB)_{\pm}\in I+\partial C\big(L^{r}(\Sigma)\big).

Proof.

Suppose
f\in L^{p}(\Sigma)
and
g\in L^{q}(\Sigma)
, then
f(Hg),(Hf)g\in L^{r}(\Sigma)
, and

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\left[\left(C_{\Sigma}f(Hg)+C_{\Sigma}(Hf)g\right)\right](z)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\int_{\Sigma}f(s)\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(\int_{|s-t|>\epsilon}g(t)\frac{{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptt}{t-s}\right)\frac{{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts}{s-z}+\int_{\Sigma}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(\int_{|s-t|>\epsilon}f(s)\frac{{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts}{s-t}\right)g(t)\frac{{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptt}{t-z}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in\Sigma\\
|s-t|>\epsilon\end{subarray}}\frac{f(s)g(t)}{t-s}\left(\frac{1}{s-z}-\frac{1}{t-z}\right){\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptt=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in\Sigma\\
|s-t|>\epsilon\end{subarray}}f(s)g(t)\frac{1}{s-z}\frac{1}{t-z}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptt

\displaystyle\qquad{}=(C_{\Sigma}fC_{\Sigma}g)(z),

where we have used the fact that the Hilbert transform converges in
L^{p}(\Sigma)
and
L^{q}(\Sigma)
. Therefore, if
A_{\pm}=I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}f
and
B_{\pm}=I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}g
, (and hence
A=I+C_{\Sigma}f
,
B=I+C_{\Sigma}g
), then
(AB)(z)
is the extension of

\displaystyle(AB)_{\pm}=I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}f+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}g+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}fC_{\Sigma}^{\pm}g=I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}\left(f+g+\frac{i}{2}(f(Hg)+(Hf)g)\right)

and, since
f\in L^{p}(\Sigma),g\in L^{q}(\Sigma)
,

\displaystyle f+g+\frac{i}{2}(f(Hg)+(Hf)g)\in L^{p}(\Sigma)+L^{q}(\Sigma)+L^{r}(\Sigma),

which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Note in the case
r=1
that
\partial C(L^{1}(\Sigma))
is not contained in
L^{1}(\Sigma)
.

Remark 4.3
.

Suppose
A_{\pm}\in I+\partial C(L^{p}(\Sigma))
and suppose that
A_{\pm}^{-1}(x)
exists a.e. and lies in
I+\partial C(L^{q}(\Sigma))
,
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}\leqslant 1
. Let
A(z),B(z)
be the analytic extensions of
A_{\pm},A_{\pm}^{-1}
off
\Sigma
respectively. Then
A(z)
is invertible in
\mathbb{C}\backslash\Sigma
and
A^{-1}(z)=B(z)
. Indeed by Proposition
4.1

(AB)_{\pm}=I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}h
for some
h\in L^{p}(\Sigma)+L^{q}(\Sigma)+L^{r}(\Sigma)
, but
(AB)_{+}=(AB)_{-}=I
and so
h=C_{\Sigma}^{+}h-C_{\Sigma}^{-}h=0
. Thus
AB(z)=A(z)B(z)=I
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Suppose
v_{A},v_{B}\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
A_{\pm}
solves the RHP:

\displaystyle A_{+}(x)=A_{-}(x)v_{A}(x),\qquad\text{for $x\in\Sigma$},\qquad A_{\pm}\in I+\partial C\big(L^{p}(\Sigma)\big),

and
B_{\pm}
the RHP:

\displaystyle B_{+}(x)=B_{-}(x)v_{B}(x),\qquad\text{for $x\in\Sigma$},\qquad B_{\pm}\in I+\partial C\big(L^{p}(\Sigma)\big).

Suppose further that
v_{B}^{-1}\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
B_{\pm}^{-1}\in I+\partial C(L^{q}(\Sigma))
where
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, then if
A,B
are the analytic extensions of
A_{\pm}
and
B_{\pm}
respectively,

\displaystyle AB^{-1}=I+C_{\Sigma}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}.

Proof.

The following calculation holds almost everywhere on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle C_{\Sigma}^{+}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}=C_{\Sigma}^{-}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}+A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{C_\Sigma^+ A_-\big(v_A v_B^{-1} - I\big)B_-^{-1}}{}=C_{\Sigma}^{-}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}+A_{+}B_{+}^{-1}-A_{-}B_{-}^{-1}.

Therefore, almost everywhere on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle I+C_{\Sigma}^{+}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}-A_{+}B_{+}^{-1}=I+C_{\Sigma}^{-}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}-A_{-}B_{-}^{-1}.

(4.1)

However, using Proposition
4.1
,

\displaystyle I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}-\big(AB^{-1}\big)_{\pm}\in\partial C\big(L^{1}(\Sigma)\big).

Thus

\displaystyle I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}-\big(AB^{-1}\big)_{\pm}=C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}h

for some
h\in L^{1}(\Sigma)+L^{p}(\Sigma)+L^{q}(\Sigma)
. However, (
4.1
) then implies that
h\equiv 0
, and therefore

\displaystyle I+C_{\Sigma}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}-AB^{-1}\equiv 0

completing the proof.
∎

Proposition
4.4
clearly implies for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\Sigma
,

\displaystyle A(z)=B(z)+\big[C_{\Sigma}A_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}\big](z)B(z)

\displaystyle\hphantom{A(z)}{}=B(z)+\big[C_{\Sigma}B_{-}\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}\big](z)B(z)

\displaystyle\hphantom{A(z)=}{}+\big[C_{\Sigma}(A_{-}-B_{-})\big(v_{A}v_{B}^{-1}-I\big)B_{-}^{-1}\big](z)B(z).

If it is possible to control the
L^{2}(\Sigma)
difference
A_{-}-B_{-}
then quantities related to
A
away from the contour
\Sigma
can be estimated by quantities that only rely on
B
and the jump functions
v_{A}
,
v_{B}
. The results of this section will lay the groundwork of controlling this difference, with the remainder of the work being done in the following section.

For compact
\Sigma
and
f\in L^{p}(\Sigma)
,
p\geqslant 1
, we have that
(C_{\Sigma}f)(z)=O\big(\frac{1}{z}\big)
as
z\to\infty
. The contour
\Sigma
defined in Section
3
is clearly compact, we will use this fact repeatedly without further comment.

4.2
The resolvent bounds

Let
h,f
refer to matrix valued functions,
h\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
f\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
, and define the operator
C_{h}
acting on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
by

\displaystyle(C_{h}f)(x)=C_{\Sigma}^{-}(f(h-I))(x)=\lim_{z\to x^{-}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{f(s)(h(s)-I)}{s-z}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\qquad\text{for $x\in\Sigma$}.

Clearly
C_{h}
is bounded from
L^{2}(\Sigma)\to L^{2}(\Sigma)
. The importance of this definition lies in its role within the general theory of Riemann–Hilbert problems, see for example
[
4
,
10
]
and the references therein. In particular, let
\mu
be any
L^{2}(\Sigma)
solution of the equation

\displaystyle(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}})\mu=I.

(4.2)

Note that
I\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
since
\Sigma
is bounded. If
1-C_{v_{\Sigma}}
is an invertible operator from
L^{2}(\Sigma)
to
L^{2}(\Sigma)
, then

\displaystyle\mu=(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}})^{-1}I

(4.3)

is the unique solution to (
4.2
). In this case the unique solution
X
to the RHP normalized at infinity with jump
v_{\Sigma}
is given by

\displaystyle X(z)=I+C_{\Sigma}(\mu(v_{\Sigma}-I)).

Our
first
step to controlling the difference
Q-\tilde{Q}
, will be showing that the resolvent in (
4.3
) is bounded uniformly of
n
for the following modified version of the Legendre Riemann–Hilbert problem:

Define, as in (
3.13
), for the same contour
\Sigma
, and subcontours
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
as depicted in Fig.
2
,

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}=\begin{cases}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\phi^{-2n}(s)&1\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}$},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
2\phi^{-2n}(s)&1\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in[1,1+\delta]$},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in[-1,1]$},\end{cases}

then we have the following theorem:

Theorem 4.5
.

The operator
1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}}
is invertible for all
n\geqslant 0
. Moreover
(1-C_{\hat{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}
is bounded uniformly of
n
in operator norm from
L^{2}(\Sigma)\to L^{2}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

The following proposition follows from Proposition 2.14 from
[
10
]
, see also
[
4
]
.

Proposition 4.6
.

A family of operators
(1-C_{h_{n}})^{-1}
is bounded uniformly with respect to
n
if and only if the inhomogeneous RHP

\displaystyle m_{+}(s)=m_{-}(s)h_{n}(s)+g(s)\qquad\text{for $s\in\Sigma$},\quad m_{\pm}\in\partial C\big(L^{2}(\Sigma)\big)

is
(
uniquely
)
solvable for all
g\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
with
||m_{\pm}||_{L^{2}}\leqslant c||g||_{L^{2}}
with
c
independent of both
n
and
g
.

We want to apply Proposition
4.6
to
h_{n}=\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}
. To this end, suppose a solution
m_{\pm}(s)=m_{\pm}(g,n,s)
exists to

\displaystyle m_{+}(s)=m_{-}(s)\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}(s)+g(s)\qquad\text{for $s\in\Sigma$},\qquad m_{\pm}\in\partial C\big(L^{2}(\Sigma)\big)

and note
m(z):=(C_{\Sigma}f)(z)
for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\Sigma
is the analytic continuation of
m_{\pm}
. It will follow from the calculation below that such a solution indeed exists. For
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
, let

\displaystyle\tilde{v}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\phi^{-2n}(z)&1\end{matrix}\right)

and, in the notation of Fig.
2
, set

\displaystyle t(z)=\begin{cases}m(z)-C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}(z)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{0}$},\\
m(z)\tilde{v}(z)-C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}(z)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{1}$},\\
m(z)\tilde{v}^{-1}(z)-C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}(z)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{2}$},\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\tilde{g}(s)=\begin{cases}g(s)&\text{for $s\in\Sigma_{1}$},\\
g(s)\tilde{v}^{-1}(s)&\text{for $s\in\Sigma_{2}$},\\
g(s)\tilde{v}^{-1}(s)&\text{for $s\in[1,1+\delta]$},\\
0&\text{for $s\in[-1,1]$}.\end{cases}

Since
\tilde{v}(s)
is uniformly bounded, it is clear that
\|\tilde{g}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\leqslant c\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
, and it is also clear that
\|m_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\leqslant c\|t_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+c\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
. For
s\in\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
, or
[1,1+\delta]
, straightforward calculations will show that
t_{+}(s)=t_{-}(s)
. Therefore
t(z)
is actually analytic for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]
. Lastly, for
z\in(-1,1)
, first note that
\tilde{g}=0
, and therefore
C_{\Sigma}^{+}\tilde{g}=C_{\Sigma}^{-}\tilde{g}
, which we label
C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}
. Another calculations shows:

\displaystyle t_{+}=t_{-}\left(\begin{matrix}\left(\dfrac{\phi_{-}}{\phi_{+}}\right)^{n}&1\\
0&\left(\dfrac{\phi_{+}}{\phi_{-}}\right)^{n}\end{matrix}\right)+C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}\left(\begin{matrix}\left(\dfrac{\phi_{-}}{\phi_{+}}\right)^{n}&1\\
0&\left(\dfrac{\phi_{+}}{\phi_{-}}\right)^{n}\end{matrix}\right)+g\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\phi_{+}^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)-C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}.

So
t(z)
solves a RHP:

(a)

\displaystyle t(z)\text{ is analytic for $z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]$},

(4.4)

(b)

for
x\in(-1,1)
,

\displaystyle t_{+}(x)=t_{-}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}\left(\dfrac{\phi_{-}}{\phi_{+}}\right)^{n}&1\\
0&\left(\dfrac{\phi_{+}}{\phi_{-}}\right)^{n}\end{matrix}\right)+\hat{g}(x),

(4.5)

(c)

as
z\to\infty

\displaystyle t(z)=O_{n}\left(\frac{1}{z}\right),

(4.6)

where

\displaystyle\hat{g}=C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}\left(\begin{matrix}\left(\dfrac{\phi_{-}}{\phi_{+}}\right)^{n}&1\\
0&\left(\dfrac{\phi_{+}}{\phi_{-}}\right)^{n}\end{matrix}\right)+g\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\phi_{+}^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)-C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}.

Since the restriction of
C_{\Sigma}
to
[-1,1]
is bounded as an operator from
L^{2}(\Sigma)
to
L^{2}([-1,1])
, and since
|\phi_{\pm}(x)|=1
for
x\in[-1,1]
. It is clear that
\|\hat{g}\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}\leqslant c\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
.

Next, let

\displaystyle u(z)=t(z)\phi^{n\sigma_{3}}(z)

it is clear that
\|t_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}\leqslant\|u_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}
. The jump conditions becomes

\displaystyle u_{+}(x)=u_{-}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)+\hat{g}\phi_{+}^{n\sigma_{3}}

for
x\in[-1,1]
, and, as
z\to\infty
,

\displaystyle u(z)=t(z)\phi^{n\sigma_{3}}(z)=O_{n}\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}z^{n}+O_{n}(z^{n-1})&0\\
0&z^{-n}+O_{n}\big(z^{-n-1}\big)\end{matrix}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{u(z)}{}=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}z^{n-1}&z^{-n-1}\\
z^{n-1}&z^{-n-1}\end{matrix}\right).

In particular, a solution to the RHP described below can be transformed in a bounded fashion to the solution
m
,

\displaystyle u(z)\text{ is analytic in $\mathbb{C}\backslash[-1,1]$},

\displaystyle u_{+}(x)=u_{-}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)+\hat{g}\phi_{+}^{n\sigma_{3}}(x)\text{ for }x\in[-1,1],

\displaystyle u(z)=O_{n}\left(\begin{matrix}z^{n-1}&z^{-n-1}\\
z^{n-1}&z^{-n-1}\end{matrix}\right)\text{ as $z\to\infty$}.

This RHP can be solved as follows. Let
\hat{g}=(\hat{g}_{1},\hat{g}_{2})
and
u=(u_{1},u_{2})
where
\hat{g}_{1}
,
\hat{g}_{2}
,
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
are the column vectors of
\hat{g}
and
u
. Then

\displaystyle u_{1+}=u_{1-}+\hat{g}_{1}\phi_{+}^{n},\qquad u_{2+}=u_{2-}+u_{1-}+\hat{g}_{2}\phi_{+}^{n}.

Thus, since
u_{1}=O_{n}(z^{n-1})
as
z\to\infty
, any polynomial
p_{n-1}
with
\deg(p_{n-1})\leqslant n-1
provides a potential solution for
u_{1}
through Plemelj’s formula,

\displaystyle u_{1}(z)=p_{n-1}(z)+\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\hat{g}_{1}(s)\phi_{+}^{n}(s)}{s-z}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

(4.7)

and, since
u_{2}=O_{n}\big(z^{-n-1}\big)
, if the solution exists, it must be, again by Plemelj’s formula,

\displaystyle u_{2}=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{u_{1-}(s)+\hat{g}_{2}(s)\phi_{+}^{-n}(s)}{s-z}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

\displaystyle\hphantom{u_2}{}=-\frac{1}{z}\int_{-1}^{1}\left(u_{1-}(s)+\hat{g}_{2}(s)\phi_{+}^{-n}(s)\right)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{s}{z}\right)^{k}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts.

(4.8)

The condition
u_{2}=O_{n}\big(z^{-n-1}\big)
is therefore equivalent to a system of
n
equations for the
n
coefficients of
p_{n-1}
. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}s^{k}\big(u_{1-}(s)+\hat{g}_{2}(s)\phi_{+}^{-n}\big){\rm d}s=0\qquad\text{for $0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1$},

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}s^{k}p_{n-1}(s){\rm d}s=-\int_{-1}^{1}\big(C_{\Sigma}^{-}\hat{g}\phi_{+}^{n}\big)(s)+\hat{g}_{2}(s)\phi_{+}^{-n}{\rm d}s\qquad\text{for $0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1$}.

(4.9)

As
\det\big(\int_{-1}^{1}s^{k+j}{\rm d}s\big)_{k,j=0}^{n-1}\neq 0
, equation (
4.9
) is a system of
n
independent, linear equations for the
n
unknowns,
a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}
, where

\displaystyle p_{n-1}(s)=a_{0}+a_{1}s+\dots+a_{n-1}s^{n-1}.

It follows that the solution
u
exists and is unique. Moreover,

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}s^{k}\big(u_{1-}(s)+\hat{g}_{2}(s)\phi_{+}^{-n}\big){\rm d}s=0\qquad\text{for $0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1$},

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}P_{k}(s)\big(p_{n-1}(s)+C_{[-1,1]}^{-}(\hat{g}_{1}\phi_{+}^{n})+\hat{g}_{2}(s)\phi_{+}^{-n}\big){\rm d}s=0.

For all polynomials with
\deg(P_{k})=k\leqslant n-1

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\big(|p_{n-1}|^{2}(s)+\overline{p_{n-1}(s)}C_{[-1,1]}^{-}(\hat{g}_{1}\phi_{+}^{n})+\overline{p_{n-1}(s)}\hat{g}_{2}(s)\phi_{+}^{-n}\big){\rm d}s=0,

\displaystyle||p_{n-1}||_{L^{2}(-1,1)}^{2}=-\int_{-1}^{1}\overline{p_{n-1}(s)}\big(C_{[-1,1]}^{-}(\hat{g}_{1}\phi_{+}^{n})+\hat{g}_{2}(s)\phi_{+}^{-n}\big){\rm d}s

\displaystyle\hphantom{||p_{n-1}||_{L^2(-1,1)}^2}{}\leqslant||p_{n-1}||_{L^{2}}||C_{[-1,1]}^{-}(\hat{g}_{1}\phi_{+}^{n})+\hat{g}_{2}\phi_{+}^{-n}||_{L^{2}}.

So, using the fact that
|\phi_{+}|=1
and the boundedness of the Cauchy operator, we have

\displaystyle||p_{n-1}||_{L^{2}}\leqslant c||\hat{g}_{2}||_{L^{2}}.

Most importantly, we notice that
c
is independent of
n
. Therefore, by using the boundedness of the Cauchy transform and the fact that
|\phi_{\pm}|=1
, as well as (
4.7
) and (
4.8
), we see that,

\displaystyle||(u_{1},u_{2})_{\pm}||_{L^{2}([-1,1])}\leqslant c^{\prime\prime}||\hat{g}||_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\leqslant c^{\prime\prime\prime}||g||_{L^{2}(\Sigma)},

where all the constants are independent of
n
. Up until now, we have been assuming solutions
m
and
t
existed. However, reversing our construction,

\displaystyle t(z)=u(z)\phi^{-n\sigma_{3}}

is a solution to the RHP for
t
, (
4.4
)–(
4.6
) with
\|t_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}\leqslant\|u_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}\leqslant c\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
, and

\displaystyle m(z)=\begin{cases}t(z)+C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}(z)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{0}$},\\
(t(z)+C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}(z))\tilde{v}^{-1}(z)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{1}$},\\
(t(z)+C_{\Sigma}\tilde{g}(z))\tilde{v}(z)&\text{for $z\in\Omega_{2}$}\end{cases}

solves

\displaystyle m(z)\text{ is analytic in $\mathbb{C}\backslash\Sigma$},

\displaystyle m_{+}(s)=m_{-}(s)\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}(s)+g(s)\text{ for $s\in\Sigma$},

\displaystyle m(z)=O_{n}\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)\text{ as $z\to\infty$}

(4.10)

and satisfies
\|m_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\leqslant c\|t\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}+c\|\tilde{g}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\leqslant c\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
.

All that remains is to show
m_{\pm}(z)\in\partial C(L^{2}(\Sigma))
. To that end, let
\tilde{\mu}(s)=m_{-}(s)
and define for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\Sigma
,

\displaystyle H(z):=(C_{\Sigma}(\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}-I)+g))(z).

(4.11)

Then for
s\in\Sigma

\displaystyle H_{+}(s)=((C_{\Sigma}^{+}\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}-I)+g))(s)=((C_{\Sigma}^{-}\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}-I)+g))(s)+\tilde{\mu}(s)(\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}(s)-I))+g(s)

\displaystyle\hphantom{H_+(s)}{}=H_{-}(s)+m_{+}-m_{-}.

Therefore

\displaystyle H_{+}-m_{+}=H_{-}-m_{-}.

It follows from the same argument as in Proposition
3.2
that the function
H(z)-m(z)
is entire. From (
4.10
) and (
4.11
),
H(z)-m(z)=O_{n}\big(\frac{1}{z}\big)
as
z\to\infty
. Thus, by Liouville’s theorem,

\displaystyle m(z)\equiv H(z)=(C_{\Sigma}(\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}-I)+g))(z)

and so

\displaystyle m_{\pm}\in\partial C(L^{2}(\Sigma))

concluding the proof of Theorem
4.5
.
∎

Theorem
4.5
has, as a corollary, the equivalent result for the modified log-orthogonal problem:

Theorem 4.7
.

The operator
1-C_{v_{\Sigma}}
is uniformly invertible for all sufficiently large
n
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}=\begin{cases}\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
\left(\dfrac{F^{2}}{w}-1\right)\phi^{-2n}&0\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}$},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
\left(\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}+\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}-2\right)\phi^{-2n}&0\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in[1,1+\delta]$},\\
0&\text{for $s\in[-1,1]$}.\end{cases}

So it follows from Propositions
2.1
and
2.3
that

\displaystyle\|v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\to 0,

which, in particular, implies that

\displaystyle\|C_{v_{\Sigma}}-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\to 0.

Combined with Theorem
4.5
, we see that

\displaystyle\big\|(C_{v_{\Sigma}}-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\to 0.

(4.12)

Writing

\displaystyle\left(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}}\right)\left(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}}\right)^{-1}=1-\left(C_{v_{\Sigma}}-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}}\right)\left(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}}\right)^{-1}

(4.13)

and using (
4.12
), we see that for
n
sufficiently large, the series

\displaystyle\big(1-(C_{v_{\Sigma}}-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big)^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\big[(C_{v_{\Sigma}}-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big]^{k}

converges in operator norm and

\displaystyle\big\|\big(1-(C_{v_{\Sigma}}-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big)^{-1}\big\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leqslant C<\infty.

It follows from (
4.13
) that
(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}})^{-1}
exists and is given by

\displaystyle(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}})^{-1}=(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big(1-(C_{v_{\Sigma}}-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big)^{-1}

for
n
sufficiently large and hence

\displaystyle\|(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leqslant\big\|(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\big\|\big(1-(C_{v_{\Sigma}}-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big)^{-1}\big\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\|(1 - C_{v_\Sigma})^{-1} \|_{L^2 \to L^2}}{}\leqslant C\big\|(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}\big\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}.

So by Theorem
4.5
,
(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}})^{-1}
is bounded in operator norm uniformly for
n
sufficiently large.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

In fact, a standard RHP argument shows that
(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}})^{-1}
exists and is bounded in
L^{2}\to L^{2}
for all
n\geqslant 0
. The point of the above theorem is that the bound is independent of
n
.

4.3
A formula for
\boldsymbol{Q_{1}-\tilde{Q}_{1}}

Proposition 4.9
.

\displaystyle Q_{1}-\tilde{Q}_{1}=-\int_{\Sigma}\mu(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz,

(4.14)

where

\displaystyle\mu=(1-C_{v_{\Sigma}})^{-1}I,\qquad\tilde{\mu}=(1-C_{\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}})^{-1}I.

Proof.

The functions
\mu
and
\tilde{\mu}
are given in terms of the RHP solutions
Q
and
\tilde{Q}
, see
[
10
]
, by

\displaystyle\mu=Q_{-},\qquad\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{Q}_{-},

and so

\displaystyle Q_{\pm}=I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}(\mu(v_{\Sigma}-I)),

and it is straightforward to show that

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{\pm}^{-1}=I+C_{\Sigma}^{\pm}\big(\big(v_{\Sigma}^{-1}-I\big)\tilde{\mu}^{-1}\big).

However
\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{Q}_{-}\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
and
\det\big(\tilde{Q}_{\pm}\big)\equiv 1
, therefore
\tilde{\mu}^{-1}\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
and clearly
v_{\Sigma}^{-1}\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, and so
\tilde{Q}_{\pm}^{-1}\in I+\partial C(L^{2}(\Sigma))
. From Theorem
4.4
, we have

\displaystyle Q(z)\tilde{Q}^{-1}(z)=I+C_{\Sigma}Q_{-}\big(v_{\Sigma}\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{-1}-I\big)\tilde{Q}_{-}^{-1},

(4.15)

but

\displaystyle v_{\Sigma}\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{-1}(s)-I=\begin{cases}\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
\left(\frac{F^{2}}{w}(s)-1\right)\phi^{-2n}(s)&0\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}$}\\
\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
\left(\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(s)+\frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(s)-2\right)\phi^{-2n}(s)&0\end{matrix}\right)&\text{for $s\in 1+\delta,1]$}\\
\mathbf{0}&\text{for $s\in[-1,1]$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\hphantom{v_\Sigma\tilde{v}_\Sigma^{-1}(s) - I}{}=v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}.

(4.16)

Therefore

\displaystyle C_{\Sigma}Q_{-}\big(v_{\Sigma}\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{-1}-I\big)\tilde{Q}_{-}^{-1}=C_{\Sigma}\mu_{-}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}_{-}^{-1}=-\frac{1}{z}\int_{\Sigma}\mu_{-}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+O\big(z^{-2}\big)

as
z\to\infty
. Therefore

\displaystyle I+\frac{Q_{1}-\tilde{Q}_{1}}{z}+O\big(z^{-2}\big)=Q\tilde{Q}^{-1}(z)=I-\frac{1}{z}\int_{\Sigma}\mu_{-}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+O\big(z^{-2}\big).

So

\displaystyle Q_{1}-\tilde{Q}_{1}=-\int_{\Sigma}\mu_{-}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts,

which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

The goal of this section is to develop the machinery that allows effective comparison of the difference of the RHPs
Q
and
\tilde{Q}
. As mentioned in the introduction, the standard approach of comparing the RHPs for
Q
,
\tilde{Q}
by their quotient breaks down. Indeed, for
x\in[1,1+\delta]
,

\displaystyle\big(Q\tilde{Q}^{-1}\big)_{+}=Q_{-}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\left(\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}+\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}\right)\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
-2\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)\tilde{Q}_{-}^{-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big(Q \tilde{Q}^{-1}\big)_+}{}=Q_{-}\tilde{Q}_{-}^{-1}\tilde{Q}_{-}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\left(\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}+\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}-2\right)\phi^{-2n}&1\end{matrix}\right)\tilde{Q}_{-}^{-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big(Q \tilde{Q}^{-1}\big)_+}{}=Q_{-}\tilde{Q}_{-}\left(I+\left(\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}+\dfrac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}-2\right)\phi^{-2n}\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{Q}_{12}\tilde{Q}_{22}&-\tilde{Q}_{12}^{2}\\
\tilde{Q}_{22}^{2}&-\tilde{Q}_{12}\tilde{Q}_{22}\end{matrix}\right)_{-}\right).

So
Q\tilde{Q}^{-1}
is the solution to a standard RHP on
\Sigma
with jump

\displaystyle J:=I+\left(\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}+\frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}-2\right)\phi^{-2n}\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{Q}_{12}\tilde{Q}_{22}&-\tilde{Q}_{12}^{2}\\
\tilde{Q}_{22}^{2}&-\tilde{Q}_{12}\tilde{Q}_{22}.\end{matrix}\right)_{-}.

(4.17)

Typically, we would like to draw conclusions about
Q\tilde{Q}^{-1}
by demonstrating that
\|J-I\|_{L^{\infty}}\to 0
. However, while the jump functions associated with
Q
,
\tilde{Q}
are close in an
L^{\infty}
sense, and specifically, near the point
1
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{F^{2}}{w_{+}}(z)+\frac{F^{2}}{w_{-}}(z)-2\right)\phi^{-2n}(z)=\frac{c}{\log^{2}(|z-1|)}+O\left(\frac{1}{\log^{3}(|z-1|)}\right),\qquad\text{as $z\to 1$},

this behavior is counteracted by the singular behavior of
\tilde{Q}
. From (
3.14
), we have that
\tilde{Q}_{12},\tilde{Q}_{22}=O_{n}(\log(|z-1|))
as
z\to 1
. More is true, in fact,
\tilde{Q}_{12},\tilde{Q}_{22}=c_{n}\log(|z-1|)(1+o(1))
as
z\to 1
where
c_{n}\sim cn^{1/2}
, which we will not prove, but which can be inferred from the behavior of
\tilde{\mu}
described in Section
B.1
. Therefore, from (
4.17
), it is clear that

\displaystyle\left\|J\right\|_{L^{\infty}([1,1+\delta])}\not\to 0\qquad\text{as $n\to\infty$}.

Thus it is not clear how to proceed in an analysis of the quotient
Q\tilde{Q}^{-1}
. Ultimately, this difficulty motivated our examination of the difference
Q-\tilde{Q}
.

Remark 4.11
.

We note that formulae (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) can be used, in principle, not only to derive our basic formula (
4.14
), but also to obtain asymptotic information about the orthogonal polynomials for general values of
z
, not just for
z=\infty
. Certainly for general
z
the analysis should go through as long as
z
is at a fixed distance away from the interval
[-1,1]
, but as
z
approaches the end points
\pm 1
, in particular, technical difficulties arise that we have not yet been able to address. Also, as
z\to\infty
, one can, in principle, use (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) to obtain lower order terms for the recurrence coefficients, but again certain technical difficulties arise that we have not yet overcome.

5
Conclusion of the proof of Theorem
1.2

In this section we combine the previous results of the paper in order to prove the main result, Theorem
1.2
.

5.1
Calculation of the integral in (
4.14
)

The integral in (
4.14
) involves both
\tilde{\mu}
and
\mu
. The asymptotics of
\tilde{\mu}
are known, see
[
13
]
, or Appendix
B.1
for a summary. Theorems
4.5
and
4.7
together with the known asymptotics of
\tilde{\mu}
give us control over
\mu
in an
L^{2}(\Sigma)
sense, which will be sufficient for our purposes.

Namely, in Proposition
B.2
we prove:

Proposition 5.1
.

The functions
\mu
,
\tilde{\mu}
obey the following estimates:

\displaystyle\big|\big|\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n)}\big)\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{1/2}\log^{2}n}\right),

(5.1)

\displaystyle\big|\big|\mu^{(n)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{1/2}\log^{2}n}\right),

(5.2)

\displaystyle\big|\big|\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n)}\big)-\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}\big)\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{3/2}\log^{2}n}\right),

(5.3)

\displaystyle\big|\big|\big(\mu^{(n)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)-\big(\mu^{(n+1)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{3/2}\log^{2}n}\right).

(5.4)

Proof.

See Proposition
B.2
.
∎

Proposition
5.1
together with Theorems
4.5
and
4.7
allow us to estimate the integral in (
4.14
) by a similar integral involving only Legendre quantities. This is done through the following proposition:

Proposition 5.2
.

The integrals can be estimated by

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\mu(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+O\left(\frac{1}{n\log^{4}n}\right)

and

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\mu^{(n)}\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+\int_{\Sigma}\mu^{(n+1)}\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

\displaystyle\qquad{}=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{4}n}\right).

Proof.

Recall that
\det(\mu),\det(\tilde{\mu})=\det(Q_{-}),\det(\tilde{Q}_{-})=1
. Therefore the norms (
5.2
) and (
5.4
) also imply

\displaystyle\big|\big|(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{1/2}\log^{2}n}\right),

\displaystyle\big|\big|\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}-\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{3/2}\log^{2}n}\right),

and therefore,

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\mu(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts-\int_{\Sigma}\left(\mu-\tilde{\mu}\right)(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts,

and by (
5.1
) and (
5.2
),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\big(\mu^{-1}-\tilde{\mu}^{-1}\big){\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|\leqslant\left\|\tilde{\mu}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\left\|\mu^{-1}-\tilde{\mu}^{-1}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=O\left(\frac{1}{n\log^{4}n}\right).

So

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\mu^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+O\left(\frac{1}{n\log^{4}n}\right).

Somewhat similarly,

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\mu^{(n)}\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+\int_{\Sigma}\mu^{(n+1)}\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

\displaystyle\qquad{}=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}-\int_{\Sigma}\big(\mu^{(n)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)\big(\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}-\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}\big){\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}-\int_{\Sigma}\big(\mu^{(n)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}-\big(\mu^{(n+1)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)\big)\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts.

Now, by (
5.2
) and (
5.3
),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Sigma}\big(\mu^{(n)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)\big(\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}-\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}\big){\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leqslant\big|\big|\mu^{(n)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\big|\big|\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}-\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{4}n}\right),

and by (
5.1
) and (
5.4
),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Sigma}\big(\mu^{(n)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}-\big(\mu^{(n+1)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)\big)(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma})\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts\right|

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leqslant\big|\big|\big(\mu^{(n)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)-\big(\mu^{(n+1)}-\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\big|\big|\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}\big)\big|\big|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{4}n}\right).

Therefore, we see that

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\mu^{(n)}\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+\int_{\Sigma}\mu^{(n+1)}\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

\displaystyle\qquad{}=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big(v^{(n)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts+\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big(v^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pts

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{4}n}\right),

which completes the proof of Proposition
5.2
.
∎

The integrals in Proposition
5.2
can be computed asymptotically using the known asymptotics of
\tilde{\mu}
, yielding:

Proposition 5.3
.

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}(z)(v_{\Sigma}(z)-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}(z))\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(z){\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz=\frac{3}{16n\log^{2}n}\left(\begin{matrix}1&-i\\
-i&-1\end{matrix}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n\log^{3}n}\right).

Proof.

See Proposition
C.3
.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}(z)\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n)}(z)-\tilde{v}^{(n)}_{\Sigma}(z)\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}(z){\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}(z)\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}(z)-\tilde{v}^{(n+1)}_{\Sigma}(z)\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}(z){\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{3}{16n^{2}\log^{2}n}\left(\begin{matrix}1&-i\\
-i&-1\end{matrix}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{3}n}\right).

Proof.

See Proposition
C.4
.
∎

Combining the results of this section, we can compute the difference
Q_{1}^{(n)}-\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}
asymptotically,

Proposition 5.5
.

The difference
Q_{1}-\tilde{Q}_{1}:=Q_{1}^{(n)}-\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}
has the following asymptotic expansions

\displaystyle Q_{1}-\tilde{Q}_{1}=\frac{3}{16n\log^{2}n}\left(\begin{matrix}-1&i\\
i&1\end{matrix}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n\log^{3}n}\right),

(5.5)

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{(n)}-\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)=\frac{3}{16n^{2}\log^{2}n}\left(\begin{matrix}-1&i\\
i&1\end{matrix}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{3}n}\right).

(5.6)

Proof.

Combining Propositions
4.9
,
5.2
, and
5.3
,

\displaystyle Q_{1}-\tilde{Q}_{1}=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\mu^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}(v_{\Sigma}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma})\tilde{\mu}^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz+O\left(\frac{1}{n\log^{3}n}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{Q_1 - \tilde{Q}_1}{}=\frac{3}{16n\log^{2}n}\left(\begin{matrix}-1&i\\
i&1\end{matrix}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n\log^{3}n}\right)

demonstrating (
5.5
). Similarly, combining Propositions
4.9
,
5.2
, and
5.4
,

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{(n)}-\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n)}\big)\big(\mu^{(n)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz+\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}\big)\big(\mu^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz

\displaystyle\qquad{}=-\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n)}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}+\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big(v_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{v}_{\Sigma}^{(n+1)}\big)\big(\tilde{\mu}^{(n+1)}\big)^{-1}{\rm d}\hskip-1.00006pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptz+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{3}n}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{3}{16n^{2}\log^{2}n}\left(\begin{matrix}-1&i\\
i&1\end{matrix}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{3}n}\right)

demonstrating (
5.6
).
∎

5.2
Computing the asymptotic behavior of
\boldsymbol{a_{n}}
,
\boldsymbol{b_{n}}

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Recall that by Proposition
3.6
,

\displaystyle a_{n}-\tilde{a}_{n}=\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{11}-\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{11}\big),

\displaystyle b_{n-1}^{2}-\tilde{b}_{n-1}^{2}=\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big)\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{b_{n-1}^2 - \tilde{b}_{n-1}^2 =}{}+\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{12}\big[\big(Q_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n)}\big)_{21}-\big(\big(Q_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}-\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}^{(n+1)}\big)_{21}\big)\big].

The calculation for
a_{n}-\tilde{a}_{n}
is read directly from Proposition
5.5

\displaystyle a_{n}-\tilde{a}_{n}=\left(\frac{3}{16n^{2}\log^{2}n}\left(\begin{matrix}-1&i\\
i&1\end{matrix}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{3}n}\right)\right)_{11}

\displaystyle\hphantom{a_n - \tilde{a}_n}{}=-\frac{3}{16n^{2}\log^{2}n}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}\log^{3}n}\right).

To calculate
b_{n-1}^{2}-\tilde{b}_{n-1}^{2}
, note from (
B.6
) and (
B.10
) that as
z\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}^{(n)}=S^{(n)}(z)=R^{(n)}(z)N(z)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\tilde{Q}^{(n)}}{}=\left(1+\frac{R_{1}(z)}{n}+O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\dfrac{a+a^{-1}}{2}&\dfrac{a-a^{-1}}{2i}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\dfrac{a-a^{-1}}{-2i}&\dfrac{a+a^{-1}}{2}\end{matrix}\right).

(5.7)

From (
B.11
),

\displaystyle R^{(n)}(z)=I+\frac{R_{1}(z)}{n}+\Delta(z),

where
R_{1}
,
\Delta(z)
are analytic in for
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\Sigma_{S}
and

\displaystyle|R_{1}(z)|\leqslant\frac{c}{|z|}\text{ and }|\Delta(z)|\leqslant\frac{c}{|z|n^{2}}\qquad\text{as $z\to\infty$}.

We can write
N(z)=I+\frac{N_{1}}{z}+O\big(z^{-2}\big)
and
N(z)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}N_{11}(z)&N_{12}(z)\\
N_{21}(z)&N_{22}(z)\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. So, in particular,
\tilde{Q}_{1}=N_{1}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
and

\displaystyle N_{12}(z)=\frac{a(z)-a^{-1}(z)}{2i}=\frac{1}{2i}\left(\left(\frac{z-1}{z+1}\right)^{1/4}-\left(\frac{z+1}{z-1}\right)^{1/4}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{N_{12}(z)}{}=\frac{1}{2i}\frac{(z-1)^{1/2}-(z+1)^{1/2}}{\big(z^{2}-1\big)^{1/4}}=\frac{1}{2i}\frac{\big(1-\frac{1}{z}\big)^{1/2}-\big(1+\frac{1}{z}\big)^{1/2}}{\big(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\big)^{1/4}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{N_{12}(z)}{}=\frac{1}{2i}\left(1-\frac{1}{2z}-1-\frac{1}{2z}+O\big(z^{-2}\big)\right)\big(1+O\big(z^{-2}\big)\big)=\left(-\frac{1}{2iz}+O\big(z^{-2}\big)\right)

from which it follows that

\displaystyle\big(\tilde{Q}_{1}\big)_{12}=-\frac{1}{2i}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

(5.8)
